Regulatory Action	Approval
Approved Indication(s)/Population(s)	Treatment of ADHD in patients ages 6 years and older
Approved Dosing Regimen(s)	Pediatric Patients 6 to 12 years: Recommended starting dosage is 39.2 mg/7.8 mg orally once daily in the morning. Dosage may be increased to 52.3 mg/10.4 mg daily or decreased to 26.1 mg/5.2 mg daily after one week.
Adults and Pediatric Patients 13 to 17 years: Recommended starting dosage is 39.2 mg/7.8 mg orally once daily in the morning. Increase the dosage after one week to 52.3 mg/10.4 mg once daily.
L6: ## 1. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L7: ## Benefit-Risk Assessment Framework
L8: ## Benefit-Risk Integrated Assessment
L9: Various formulations of methylphenidate have been marketed in the United States since 1955 for the treatment of ADHD. This 505 (b)(2) application is for KP415 (Azstarys), a stimulant-class drug product that consists of two components: serdexamethylphenidate (SDX), which is an inactive prodrug of dextroamphetamine (d-MPH), and immediate-release d-MPH. The proposed indication is for the treatment of Attention Deficit-Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) in patients ages 6 years and older. The drug is proposed for once-daily administration. The Applicant proposes to market three strengths of KP415 (26.1/5.2, 39.2/7.8, and 52.3/10.4 mg, intended to be roughly equivalent to a total d-MPH dose of 20, 30, and 40 mg respectively.
L10: The Applicant provided an acceptable bridge to the listed drug Focalin XR (NDA 021802, approved in 2005), which is a bimodal-release version of d-MPH. KP415 and Focalin XR are not bioequivalent. Compared to 40 mg Focalin XR, the mean Cmax and AUCinf of d-MPH following a single dose administration of 52.3/10.4 mg KP415 were approximately 39% and 25% lower, respectively. d-MPH exposures between KP415 and Focalin XR become more comparable at steady state: mean Cmax and AUC0-24hr of 52.3/10.4 mg KP415 were about 71% and 87% of 40 mg Focalin XR, respectively.
L11: Considering the prior finding of efficacy of Focalin XR for the proposed indication, a single efficacy study with KP415 would be sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. The Applicant conducted such study in patients 6 to 12 years of age (Study KP415.E01). The study used a double-blind, dose-optimized, randomized design. It consisted of a 3-week open-label treatment period followed by a 1-week placebo-controlled double-blind phase with lab classroom comparison on Day 28. The primary endpoint was the Swanson, Kotkin, Agler, M-Flynn, and Pelham rating scale (SKAMP), which has been used in prior FDA approvals for ADHD indications. The SKAMP is a validated rating of impairment of classroom behaviors in children with ADHD. Study KP415.E01 enrolled 150 patients (74 on KP415 and 76 on placebo), and clearly established the efficacy of KP415 in that population. However, because of the allowed individual patient titration, the study provides limited dose-response information. Efficacy in patients older than those evaluated in Study KP415.E01 (i.e., adolescent and adult patients) is established by a finding that the 52.3/10.4 mg dosage of KP415 provides d-MPH exposure that are, at every daytime timepoint, at least as high as those with Focalin XR 20 mg, an effective dosage for adult patients.
L12: ## Summary Review
L13: Safety findings for KP415 were generally comparable to approved methylphenidate products. It is noteworthy that the d-MPH exposures after KP415 administration at the highest proposed dosage are no higher than those observed after administration of Focalin XR 40 mg daily, so that the pharmacokinetic bridging allows to rely on the Focalin XR safety information.
L14: KP415 offers no distinct advantages over currently approved MPH products.
L15: ## Benefit-Risk Dimensions
L16: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Attention Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) is the most common neurobehavioral disorder of childhood, with a lifetime prevalence in the pediatric population of about 11%. It typically presents in early school years and is characterized by difficulty paying attention, hyperactivity, and impulsive behavior. These symptoms cause significant impairment in academic and social functioning during critical years of development, unless treated.
ADHD is a very prevalent condition in children and adolescents. ADHD symptoms can substantially compromise childhood academic and social development without treatment.
Current Treatment Options	Numerous drugs have been approved for the treatment of ADHD. The most common are stimulants, containing amphetamine salts or methylphenidate. Approved non-stimulant medications include atomoxetine or guanfacine.
Patients with ADHD have individual needs regarding onset and duration of medication effect and frequency of dosing, such as during school or work.
Patients also have individual needs regarding ease of administration, particularly children who cannot always swallow large pills whole.
KP415 was meant to address some of these concerns for the treatment of ADHD, but offers no distinct advantages over currently approved MPH products.
Benefit	KP415 is a capsule formulation that can be either swallowed whole or sprinkled onto applesauce or into water.
Study KP415.E01 was a placebo-controlled efficacy and safety laboratory classroom study in children ages 6 to 12 years. In the study, patients initially entered a 3-week open-label dose optimization phase with a KP415 dose of 39.2 mg SDX/7.8 mg d-MPH taken once each morning. The dose could be decreased to 26.1/5.2 mg to improve tolerability or increased to 52.3/10.4 mg to improve effectiveness. After a 2-day washout for baseline efficacy assessments, patients continued either their current optimized KP415 dose or placebo at Day 21 for 7 days before a laboratory classroom day on Day 28. The primary endpoint was the average change from baseline to Day 28 on the SKAMP. The SKAMP is a validated rating of impairment of classroom behaviors in children with ADHD. KP415 was significantly better than
The safety and efficacy of KP415 in children ages 6 to 12 years was demonstrated in a clinical study, which showed that once-daily dosing with KP415 provides up to 9 hours of effect in a classroom setting.
Efficacy in adolescent and adult patients is established by a PK bridge to Focalin XR (as listed drug).
L17: ## Summary Review
L18: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
placebo for the primary outcome measure on Day 28, with a LS mean average change from baseline treatment difference of -5.4 [(95% CI: -7.1, -3.7); p<0.001] between KP415 and placebo.
KP415's PK profile allows for once-daily dosing. Per the prespecified secondary analysis, the onset of action occurred at 1 hour post-dose (peaking at 2 hours post-dose), with action for up to 10 hours post-dose (total duration of action of 9 hours during the lab classroom day).
Efficacy in adolescent and adult patients is established by a PK bridge to Focalin XR. PK data show that d-MPH blood levels with KP415 52.3/10.4 mg are as least as high at all daily time points as those with Focalin XR 20 mg per day, an effective dose of Focalin XR (Focalin XR label notes that "there was no obvious increase in effectiveness with increasing the dose" [between 20 mg and 40 mg]).

Risk and Risk Management	Adverse events (AEs) in clinical studies in patients 6 to 12 years of age include decreased appetite, mood changes, insomnia, abdominal pain, headache, and behavioral changes.
Mild elevations in mean blood pressure and pulse rate, and decreases in weight gain and growth, were noted during open-label Study KP415.S01, relative to standard mean Z-scores. These events are common with this drug class.
Possible hypersensitivity reactions to KP415 include bronchospasm, rash, and pruritus.
A higher than expected rate of markedly abnormal mean values on laboratory serum coagulation times and urine proteinuria was observed in the open-label clinical study. However, a relationship to treatment appears unlikely.
Safety in patients 12 years of age and older is established by an acceptable PK bridge to Focalin XR.
KP415 did not demonstrate any major unexpected safety findings other than those already known for other approved MPH products in clinical studies in patients ages 6-12 years.
Safety in older age groups is established by an acceptable PK bridge to Focalin XR.
L19: ## 2. Background
L20: - KP415 (Azstarys) is a combination stimulant-class drug product consisting of two components: serdexamethylpenidate (SDX), which is an inactive prodrug of dexamethylphenidate (d-MPH), and immediate-release d-MPH. SDX is converted to d-MPH in the gastrointestinal tract. The immediate-release d-MPH component is intended to produce therapeutic blood levels of d-MPH during the first few hours after administration of KP415. Originally, KP415 was formulated solely as the prodrug SDX. However, early studies found an unexpected long time to onset of clinical effect, and the Applicant decided to combine the drug with immediate-release d-MPH, which has an earlier onset of action.
- This 505(b)(2) application uses Focalin XR as listed drug.
- Please refer to the Unireview document for more complete background information on KP415.
L21: ## 3. Product Quality
L22: The Office of Product Quality recommends approval for this application. There are no outstanding product quality issues.
L23: ## 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L24: The nonclinical studies conducted by the Applicant did not identify any unexpected safety findings for KP415 relative to other d-MPH products. Overall, the nonclinical team concludes that the Applicant had provided sufficient nonclinical safety information to support approval in patients ages 6 years and older, and recommends approval for this application.
L25: ## 5. Clinical Pharmacology
L26: Azstarys (KP415) is an oral, once-daily capsule that comprises immediate-release SDX, a prodrug of d-MPH, and immediate-release d-MPH. It is formulated in a fixed molar dose ratio of 70% SDX chloride (Cl) to 30% d-MPH hydrochloride (HCl). Chemically, SDX consists of a single d-MPH molecule covalently attached via a carbamate bond to a methylene oxide linker, which in turn is connected to the nitrogen of the pyridine ring of a nicotinoyl-serine moiety.
L27: The final to-be-marketed formulation has been developed in three dosage strengths: 26.1/5.2 mg; 39.2/7.8 mg; 52.3/10.4 mg SDX/d-MPH (free-base weight), corresponding to total molar equivalent doses of 20, 30, and 40 mg Focalin XR, respectively.
L28: Ten clinical pharmacology studies were conducted in the development program, assessing either SDX Cl alone or the SDX Cl/d-MPH HCl combination (KP415). Pivotal PK studies
L29: include: a mass balance study with ^{14}\text{C}-labeled SDX Cl (KP415.111); a single-dose, relative bioavailability study comparing SDX/d-MPH to the LD, Focalin XR (KP415.107); a single-dose, dose-proportionality study, followed by a multiple-dose, steady-state phase with the highest dosage strength (KP415.110); a food effect study with highest dosage strength (KP415.104); and a PK study in pediatric and adolescent patients with ADHD (KP415.105). Additionally, PK information have also been obtained from three human abuse liability (HAP) studies where SDX was administered orally (KP415.A01), intranasally (KP415.A02), and intravenously (KP415.A03). QT-Concentration relationship was explored in the program. In vitro studies using human biomaterials were conducted to evaluate epithelial cell permeability, plasma protein binding, hepatic metabolism, and metabolism- and transporter-based drug-drug interaction potential of SDX.
L30: ## Absorption:
L31: Mean maximal plasma concentrations of SDX and d-MPH were reached approximately 2 hours after oral dosing of KP415 under fasted conditions. Following administration of 52.3/10.4 mg KP415 and 40 mg Focalin XR in healthy volunteers under fasted conditions (see Figure 1):
L32: - The mean peak plasma concentration (C_{\text{max}}) of d-MPH was 14.0 ng/mL and 28.2 ng/mL, respectively;
- The mean area under concentration curve (AUC) of d-MPH was 186 hour*ng/mL and 248 hour*ng/mL, respectively.
L33: ## Distribution:
L34: Plasma protein binding of SDX and d-MPH was approximately 56% and 47%, respectively, at 5 \mu\text{M} in vitro. Mean volume of distribution of SDX was 29.3 L/kg after KP415 dosing; mean volume of distribution d-MPH was 2.65 L/kg.
L35: ## Elimination:
L36: The mean plasma terminal elimination half-life of SDX and d-MPH was about 5.7 hours and 11.7 hours, respectively, following administration of a single dose of 52.3/10.4 mg KP415 in healthy adults. The mean oral clearance of SDX was 3.6 L/hr/kg after KP415 dosing. Dexmethylphenidate was eliminated with a mean clearance of 0.40 L/hr/kg after intravenous administration.
L37: ## Metabolism:
L38: Conversion of SDX to d-MPH most likely takes place mainly in the lower gastrointestinal tract, and enzymes involved in the process are yet to be identified.
L39: Dexmethylphenidate is metabolized primarily via de-esterification to d-\alpha-phenyl-piperidine acetic acid (also known as d-ritalinic acid). This metabolite has little or no pharmacological activity.
L40: ## Excretion:
L41: After oral dosing of radiolabeled SDX (single moiety) in humans, approximately 0.4% and 11% of the dose was excreted unchanged in urine and feces, respectively. The main urinary
L42: and fecal metabolite of SDX was ritalinic acid (RA), accounting for approximately 63% of the total recovered dose.
L43: After oral dosing of radiolabeled racemic methylphenidate, about 90% of the radioactivity was recovered in urine, with RA accounting for approximately 80% of the dose.
L44: ## **Special Populations:**
L45: Dosage adjustments are not recommended for hepatic or renal impairment, or based on race or gender. However, d-MPH exposures in adolescents and adults are about 50% of those seen in children after similar dose administration (see Figure 2).
L46: ## **QT Effect:**
L47: At a mean concentration 40 times the C_{max} for the highest dose of KP415 (52.3/10.4 mg base equivalent), SDX does not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.
L48: ## **Food Effect:**
L49: Although the presence of food (sprinkled on applesauce or high-fat, high calorie food) decreased the absorption and exposure of SDX to varying degrees, the exposure to d-MPH, the active moiety, was increased to a maximum of 33% for Cmax and 16% for AUC, compared to fasted conditions. Minimal effect was observed on d-MPH exposure when KP415 capsule was opened and content was added to water.
L50: **Figure 1: Mean Concentration Time Profiles of d-MPH Following Administration of KP415 or Focalin XR under Fasted Conditions**
L51: Source: KP415.107 CSR
L52: (Note the SDX/d-MPH dose is listed in salt form, with 56/12 mg equivalent to 52.3/10.4 mg free base)
L53: Similar PK profile shapes were demonstrated in children 6 to 12 years old, adolescents, and adults, with linear PK. The exposure of d-MPH in adolescents and adults is, however, about 50% of that in children 6 to 12 years old, after administration of the same dose KP415 (see Figure 2). A similar observation was made for Focalin XR, which is the listed drug for this 505(b)(2) application. With Focalin XR, after administration of the same dose to pediatric
L54: patients and adults, concentrations in pediatric patients were approximately twice the concentrations observed in adults.
L55: **Figure 2: Plasma d-MPH Concentration Time Profiles Across Age Groups Following A Single Dose Administration of 52.3/10.4 mg KP415**
L56: *Data in 6-12 age group contain dose-normalized concentration from 26.1/5.2 mg dose in children 6-8 years old and 52.3/10.4 mg dose in children 9-12 years old.
L57: -Source: reviewer analysis
L58: KP415 at 52.3/10.4 mg (the proposed maximum recommended dose) at steady state provides d-MPH plasma levels at least as high as those after administration of Focalin XR 20 mg, at all daily time points (see Figure 3 and Figure 4). The 20 mg dose of Focalin XR is an effective dose, as described in the Focalin XR label.

L66: ## 6. Clinical Microbiology
L67: N/A
L68: ## 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L69: Study KP415.E01 and a pharmacokinetic bridge to the Focalin XR NDA together provide substantial evidence of the effectiveness of KP415 in the treatment of patients with ADHD 6 years of age and older.
L70: ## Pediatric patients 6 to 12 years of age
L71: Study KP415.E01 was a multicenter randomized trial in patients ages 6 to 12 years with ADHD (see Figure 5). The study consisted of three phases: 1) a screening period of up to 49 days, 2) an open-label dose-optimization phase of around 3 weeks with daily dosing of KP415 (d-MPH equivalent of 30 mg), which could be adjusted to d-MPH equivalents of 20 mg or 40 mg as needed, 3) a double-blind placebo-controlled treatment phase of 1 week, with efficacy endpoint measured on Day 28 in a laboratory classroom setting, and 4) a follow-up visit around 3 days post-final dose.
L72: The primary endpoint was the Swanson, Kotkin, Agler, M-Flynn, and Pelham rating scale (SKAMP), which has been used in prior FDA approvals for ADHD indications. The SKAMP is a validated rating of impairment of classroom behaviors in children with ADHD.
L73: Figure 5: Design of Study KP415.E01
L74: Source: Figure 1 in the Clinical Study Report for Study KP415.E01
L75: Per Protocol Amendment 2 for this study, the primary efficacy endpoint was the difference between KP415 and placebo in the average of the mean changes from baseline on the SKAMP combined score across all post dose time points (up to Hour 13 post-dose) on the classroom day (Day 28). The protocol defined the baseline as the predose score on Day 21, and the intent-to-treat population as all randomized patients who received at least one dose of double-blind study medication and have at least one post-dose SKAMP-C assessment on Day 28.
L76: The SAP states that demonstration of statistical significance on the primary efficacy endpoint would permit analysis of the mean changes from baseline in the SKAMP-C at each time point during the classroom day as secondary efficacy endpoints, and would also permit use of these data to estimate onset and duration of clinical efficacy.
L77: After the Applicant unblinded and analyzed the efficacy data, they requested that the Agency consider a revision of the baseline definition as a post-hoc analysis. They proposed that the baseline be defined as the SKAMP-C assessment at the predose timepoint on the classroom day (Day 28) for purposes of analyzing the primary endpoint and estimating the onset of action and duration of effect. The reason for this proposal was the Applicant's opinion that, in retrospect, the protocol-specified analysis did not reflect the true magnitude of the time-dependent differences over the course of the classroom day, and they had selected the wrong baseline. (We had already informed the Applicant during an End-of-Phase 1 Meeting that the baseline selected was somewhat atypical, but the Applicant did not change the endpoint at that time.)
L78: We agreed at a meeting with the Applicant shortly before NDA submission to consider the proposed post-hoc analysis, but noted that it would be a matter of review. After further consideration, we do not find the Applicant's rationale sufficient to justify changing the prespecified analysis for the primary efficacy endpoint.
L79: A total of 150 children (approximately 60% male and 40% female) completed Study KP415.E01
L80: The primary efficacy results are described in Table 1 and Figure 6. Using either the protocol-specified analysis (Day 21 baseline) or post-hoc analysis proposed by the Applicant (Day 28 baseline), the average decrease in the SKAMP-C score across all post-baseline time points on Day 28 was greater among KP415-treated patients than among placebo-treated patients to a statistically significant degree. There was no nominally significant difference between drug and placebo past Hour 10 for the prespecified analysis of individual timepoints (see Figure 5), whereas for the post-hoc analysis, there was a nominal statistical difference between the groups until Hour 13.

L89: It is worth noting that 51% of the 150 patients remaining in the study at the end of dose optimization were on the d-MPH equivalent of 40 mg (and only 3% on the 20-mg equivalent dose, and 46% on the 30-mg equivalent), with the mean dose around 34 mg/day. This data seems to indicate that doses in the higher range (30 to 40 mg) were the most commonly used to achieve efficacy (with no major tolerability issues). However, dose response cannot be formally evaluated with a flexible dose study design, and a fixed-dose study would be needed to conclusively understand dose-response for efficacy. That evaluation, which is needed to identify the lowest effective dose, will be requested in the new pediatric study that will be conducted as a PMR (see below).
L90: ## Adolescent and adult patients
L91: The Applicant did not conduct a clinical efficacy study in adolescents or in adult patients. The review team therefore examined whether other data could support efficacy of KP415 in older age groups (adolescents and adults), e.g., by extrapolation from the finding of efficacy in patients 6 to 12 years of age in Study KP415.E01. A concern from the review team was that KP415 and Focalin XR are not bioequivalent, with overall d-MPH exposure levels generally lower with KP415 than with equivalent doses of Focalin XR, and mean d-MPH C_{max} and AUC in adolescents and adults who received KP415 only about half of the respective values in children. The Applicant asserted that scalability of dosing could address this difference in exposure. However, exposure with the highest proposed dose of KP415.E01 is lower than the 40 mg dose of Focalin XR, and so the data do not support that the full range of exposure that may be obtained with Focalin XR in adolescents and adults can be reproduced with KP415.
L92: The PK bridge between KP415 and Focalin XR is, however, sufficient to establish the efficacy of KP415 in adolescent and adult patients for the proposed indication. The effective dose of Focalin XR for the treatment ADHD in adults, as described in labeling, ranges between 20 mg and 40 mg, with a comment in labeling that “All three Focalin XR doses were statistically significantly superior to placebo. There was no obvious increase in effectiveness with increasing dose.” As the 52.3/10.4 mg dosage of KP415 provides d-MPH exposures that are, at every daytime timepoint, at least as high as those with Focalin XR 20 mg, a conclusion of efficacy of KP415 52.3/10.4 mg in adult and adolescent patients can be reached.
L93: ## *Conclusions on efficacy:*
L94: The Applicant has provided an acceptable PK bridge between KP415 and Focalin XR. The drugs, however, are not bioequivalent, and d-MPH exposure at the highest proposed dose of KP415 is lower than that at the highest recommended dose of Focalin XR (40 mg).
L95: Considering the prior finding of efficacy of Focalin XR for the proposed indication, a single efficacy study with KP415 would be sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. The Applicant conducted such study in patients 6 to 12 years of age, and that provides substantial evidence of efficacy in that age group.
L96: Efficacy in older patients is established by a finding that the 52.3/10.4 mg dosage of KP415 provides d-MPH exposure that are, at every daytime timepoint, at least as high as those with Focalin XR 20 mg, an effective dosage for the proposed indication.
L97: ## 8. Safety
L98: A total of 374 children and adolescents with ADHD, and 120 healthy adult volunteers, were exposed to KP415 in phase 1 and phase 3 studies. Efficacy Study KP415.E01 enrolled 155 patients and, of these, 94 entered open-label extension Study KP415.S01 (of 12 months duration). Another 188 patients enrolled as *de novo* patients in Study KP415.S01 (238 patients in total). All patients in Study KP415.E01, and most patients in Study KP415.S01, had 3 weeks of dose-optimization treatment at the beginning of the study (see above for description of the design of Study KP415.E01).
L99: During the dose optimization phase of Study KP415.S01, AEs reported in at least 5% of patients were: decreased appetite (19%), insomnia (13%), and mood changes (13%). These events are similar to those reported during the dose optimization phase of Study KP415.E01. The most common AEs observed in Study KP415.E01 (after the dose-optimization phase) include: decreased appetite (25%), mood changes (24%), insomnia (17%), abdominal pain (10%), headache (8%), and behavioral changes (5%).
L100: During the 12-month treatment phase of Study KP415.S01, the following AEs were reported in at least 5% of patients: upper respiratory tract infection (22%), decreased appetite (19%), mood changes (11%), insomnia (9%), weight decreased (8%), headache (5%), and weight increased (5%). Generally, these AEs were consistent with those described in Focalin XR's labeling. Only compulsive behaviors (2%) were reported more frequently in KP415 studies
L101: than in Focalin XR studies, but given the study design, a conclusive comparison is not possible.
L102: There were no deaths in the KP415 development program. There were four serious AEs (SAEs), with three occurring in Study KP415.S01, and one in an intravenous abuse potential study: aggressive behavior, seizure, asthma/hyperglycemia, and motor vehicle accident. Only the aggressive behavior SAE appears potentially related to study drug, and led to study drug discontinuation.
L103: Dropouts due to AEs (4 in Study KP415.E01 and 11 in Study KP415.S01) either appeared related to issues known with MPH-containing drugs, or were not clearly attributable to the study drug. No QT prolongation issues were noted with this drug. No clear signal for suicidal ideation and behavior events was noted, although 8 patients (3%) in Study S01 had suicidal behavior events (with none reported as AEs). With no placebo comparator arm, interpreting these events is difficult.
L104: During Study KP415.S01, the Children's Sleep Habits Questionnaire (CSHQ) was used to track sleep behavior; mean changes from baseline over time noted mild reduction in scores, although scores were still elevated overall, in the 44 to 45 range (with scores 41 or higher indicate sleep problems). The incidence of insomnia AEs in Study KP415.E01 (17%) or Study KP415.S01 (13%) are relatively high, but comparison to other MPH or long-acting stimulant drugs is not possible due to the lack of controlled data and to differences in study design. A few cases of bronchospasm, rash, and pruritus were noted as possible hypersensitivity reactions to KP415, and the relevant information will be included in Section 4 of labeling.
L105: Mean blood pressure and heart rate increases were observed in KP415 studies, and a notable proportion of patients had markedly abnormal elevations in BP (8% SBP/5% DBP in Study E01, and 16% SBP/19% DBP in S01). Growth rate and weight decreases were observed in Study S01, as expected with this drug class. Due to the lack of placebo control, and difference in study design, the review team was unable to compare these findings with those of other MPH products. None of the aforementioned issues led to serious AEs, hospitalizations, or deaths.
L106: The only somewhat unexpected findings on the laboratory parameters from Study E01 and S01 were higher rates than expected of patients with markedly abnormal coagulation times and urine proteinuria. One AE of easy bruising was noted in a patient from Study S01. No urinary AEs were noted. Considering the extent of safety information available for d-MPH-containing products, and the absence of such adverse reactions for these products, it appears very unlikely that these events were drug-related. It is notable that d-MPH exposure with KP415 does not exceed that with the highest dose of Focalin XR, so that reliance on the prior finding of long-term safety of Focalin XR is reassuring.
L107: As d-MPH exposure with the proposed maximum approved dose of KP415 (52.3/10.4 mg) is lower in adults and adolescents than in pediatric patients age 6 to 12 years, and given that the overall safety profile of d-MPH has been well-studied in all age groups (including the general safety results of Study E01, albeit non-controlled, and safety data from the LD Focalin XR),
L108: with an adequate PK bridge to the Focalin XR NDA, the safety of KP415 is also established in adolescents and in adult patients.
L109: Overall, the safety profile of KP415 is acceptable, and similar to that of approved MPH products.
L110: ## 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L111: This application was not referred for review to an advisory committee because the safety profile of KP415 is acceptable for the intended population, the clinical trial designs were acceptable, and the efficacy findings were clear.
L112: ## 10. Pediatrics
L113: An Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP), dated August 1, 2018, addresses the KP415 pediatric clinical studies by the following age ranges:
L114: 0 to <4 years	A waiver will be requested because studies in this age range are highly impractical.
L115: (b) (4)
L116: As described in the iPSP, studies in pediatric patients ages 4 to &lt;6 years are needed. In addition, dose response could not be formally evaluated in Study E01 because of the flexible dose design. Further information would be useful to better characterize the dose response for safety and efficacy in pediatric patients ages 6 to 12 years, and also to interpret the safety data that will be obtained in pediatric patients ages 4 years to &lt;6 years. That information will be obtained in the new pediatric study that will be requested as a PMR. That study will be
L117: conducted in patients 4 to 12 years of age diagnosed with ADHD, with randomization stratified by age group (i.e., 4 to less than 6 years, and 6 to 12 years of age).
L118: The Pediatric Team in the Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health (DPMH) was consulted for assistance with the pediatric assessment of this application. Based on more updated thinking from our division's ADHD guidance and from the safety results of this NDA, we determined that some adjustments to the original iPSP could be made to consolidate the studies (such as sparse PK sampling in preschool age children). The Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) discussed this application with DP on February 2, 2021. This discussion was focused on the Division's proposed PMRs at that time. The PeRC agreed with PMRs for 1) a randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group, safety and efficacy trial in patients ages 4 through 12 years; 2) a 12-month, open-label safety study in patients ages 4 to less than 6 years; and 3) a randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group, safety and efficacy study in adolescent patients ages 13 through 17 years. Subsequent to that meeting, DP concluded that adequate safety and efficacy data existed to support approval in adolescent patients; therefore, the last PMR was dropped from further consideration.
L119: ## 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L120: - Controlled Substances Staff (CSS): KP415 should be classified with similar scheduling (Schedule II) and abuse potential as other MPH stimulant products. Recent abuse potential studies that the Applicant conducted with SDX only will not be included in labeling, as they do not address the properties of the combination product.
- Due to the COVID-19 pandemic restricting travel, OSI was unable to complete clinical site inspections for this NDA. These inspections are not critical for this application, considering the acceptable bridge to Focalin XR as listed drug, and the extensive knowledge about this drug class and about d-MPH.
L121: ## 12. Labeling
L122: ## *Prescribing Information*
L123: - General descriptive safety results from Studies E01 and S01 along with class labeling and AE findings from other MPH products will be included in labeling.
- Efficacy data from the prespecified primary and secondary efficacy analyses will be included in Section 14. (b) (4)
L124: ## 13. Postmarketing Recommendations
L125: ## *Postmarketing Requirements (PMRs) and Commitments (PMCs)*
L126: ## **PREA PMR 3980-1**
L127: Conduct a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group, safety and efficacy study of Azstarys in male and female children 4 to 12 years of age diagnosed with ADHD. Randomization should be stratified by two age groups (i.e., 4 to less than 6 years, and 6 to 12 years of age). Also, this study must include sparse pharmacokinetic (PK) sampling in children ages 4 to less than 6 years to characterize the shape of the PK curve in this population.
L128: ## **PREA PMR 3980-2**
L129: Conduct a 12-month, open-label study to obtain information on safety and tolerability of Azstarys in male and female children 4 to less than 6 years of age diagnosed with ADHD.

L169: ## 1 Executive Summary
L170: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L171: KP415 is a combination drug product consisting of two components: 1) serdexamethylphenidate (SDX), which is an inactive prodrug of dexmethylphenidate (d-MPH), and 2) immediate-release d-MPH. SDX is converted to d-MPH in the gastrointestinal tract, a process which gradually produces d-MPH in the systemic circulation, with peak concentrations approximately 8 hours after ingestion. The immediate-release d-MPH component produces therapeutic blood levels of d-MPH during the first few hours after administration.
L172: Dexmethylphenidate is the d-isomer of racemic d,l-methylphenidate. Methylphenidate is thought to block the reuptake of norepinephrine and dopamine into the presynaptic neuron and increase the release of the monoamines into the extraneuronal space, with the d-isomer being more pharmacologically active than the l-isomer. Methylphenidate was first FDA-approved for the treatment of ADHD in 1955 and it, or its d-isomer, is the active ingredient in several currently marketed products for the treatment of patients with ADHD.
L173: KP415 capsules are available in three strengths (expressed in mg of free base):
L174: - 26.1 mg SDX/5.2 mg d-MPH (equivalent to 28/6 mg as a salt and a total d-MPH dose of 20 mg).
- 39.2 mg SDX/7.8 mg d-MPH (equivalent to 42/9 mg as a salt and a total d-MPH dose of 30 mg).
- 52.3 mg SDX/10.4 mg d-MPH (equivalent to 56/12 mg as a salt and a total d-MPH dose of 40 mg).
L175: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L176: The conclusion on efficacy of KP415 relies on: 1) the established efficacy of the Listed Drug (LD), Focalin XR; 2) the pharmacokinetic (PK) bridge to the LD, demonstrated by Studies KP 415.105, KP415.107 and KP415.110; and 3) the demonstration of efficacy in children, ages 6 to 12 years, in Study KP415.E01. These data are sufficient to support a claim for the treatment of patients, 6 years of age and older, with ADHD.
L177: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L178: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L179: KP415 is a combination stimulant-class drug product consisting of two components: 1) serdexamethylphenidate (SDX), which is an inactive prodrug of dexmethylphenidate (d-MPH), and 2) immediate-release d-MPH. SDX is converted to d-MPH in the gastrointestinal tract and then eventually in the systemic circulation. The immediate-release d-MPH component produces therapeutic blood levels of d-MPH during the first few hours after administration. While various formulations of methylphenidate have been marketed in the United States since 1955 for the treatment of Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD), for regulatory purposes FDA considers KP415 a combination product with a new molecular entity (NME) for the SDX portion in combination with the d-MPH IR portion. The d-MPH IR portion is relying upon 505(b)(2) bridging with the listed drug Focalin XR (NDA 021802, approved in 2005) which is a bimodal-release version of d-MPH. The Applicant plans to market three strengths of KP415 (26.1/5.2, 39.2/7.8, 52.3/10.4 mg) equivalent to a total d-MPH dose of 20, 30, and 40 mg respectively.
L180: The Applicant conducted one new pediatric efficacy and safety study (KP415.E01) for this NDA submission utilizing a double-blind, dose-optimized, randomized lab classroom design in 150 patients (74 on KP415 and 76 on placebo) ages 6 to 12 with a 3-week open-label treatment period and 1-week placebo-controlled double-blind phase with lab classroom comparison only on Day 28. The efficacy of KP415 relies on: 1) the established efficacy of the Listed Drug (LD), Focalin XR; 2) the pharmacokinetic (PK) bridge to the LD, demonstrated by Studies KP415.105, KP415.107, and KP415.110; and 3) the demonstration of efficacy in children, ages 6 to 12, in Study KP415.E01. These data are sufficient to support a claim for the treatment of patients, age 6 years and older, with ADHD. There was also an open-label long-term (12-month) pediatric safety study KP415.S01 as an extension from Study KP415.E01 and additional safety data from other phase 1 studies.
L181: Together with PK bridging to the LD, we agree that statistically significant results on an established endpoint (the SKAMP) in one novel study (KP415.E01) is sufficient to establish the efficacy of KP415 for the treatment of ADHD in patients age 6 years and older. There were disagreements with the Applicant on which statistical analysis to include [REDACTED](b)(4); the Applicant preferred a post-hoc analysis utilizing a new baseline timepoint (morning of Day 28 instead of Day 21) that our team felt would not be as scientifically sound as the original prespecified one and potentially misrepresented the drug's effects relative to placebo. [REDACTED](b)(4). The effective daily doses in children were 39.2 mg SDX/7.8 mg d-MPH and 52.3 mg SDX/10.4 mg d-MPH (with more than half requiring the higher dose), and the maximum dose the Applicant has proposed for KP415 labeling is 52.3 mg SDX/10.4 mg d-MPH. Extrapolation of efficacy to adolescents and adults is based on the finding of equal or higher d-MPH concentrations, consistently at all time points, after KP415 52.3 mg SDX/10.4 mg d-MPH/day at steady state compared to single dose Focalin XR 20 mg in adults. Downward extrapolation from adults to adolescents is reasonable based on the similarity of d-MPH PK between adults and adolescents. Because evidence
L182: of efficacy in adults and adolescents at KP415 dosages of 26.1 mg SDX/5.2 mg d-MPH and 39.2 mg SDX/7.8 mg d-MPH/day is not available, the recommended daily dose for adolescents and adults is 52.3 mg SDX/10.4 mg d-MPH.
L183: Safety findings in KP415.E01 and the other open-label studies were generally comparable to existing approved methylphenidate products. The only unusual findings were some laboratory values indicating higher than expected coagulation times and proteinuria rates on drug; neither concern is a known issue for MPH-related drugs. After reviewing additional Applicant data and consulting our pediatric reviewers, our team felt there was no conclusive safety signal related to the drug. We also remain concerned that we do not have sufficient placebo-controlled safety data from the submitted studies, even for the age 6 to 12 range, given that SDX is still technically an NME. Accordingly, we will recommend that the Sponsor conduct an efficacy and safety parallel-group, placebo-controlled study for children ages 4 to 12 years (as we also will require similar data for the preschool age population per our division's guidance on ADHD drug development). Because d-MPH exposures in adolescents and adults are only approximately 50% of those in children, it is reasonable to assess safety in adolescents and adults based on the safety evaluation of KP415 in children.
L184: Overall, we note that KP415 administered once daily is effective and safe for the treatment of ADHD in patients age 6 years and older and recommend approval for this indication. However, because 1) ADHD is prevalent in children, ages 4 and 5 years, and there are no PK, safety, or efficacy data in those patients; and 2) there are no placebo-controlled safety data in children 6 to 12 years of age, the Applicant will be required, as Postmarketing Requirements (PMRs), to conduct 1) a randomized, placebo-controlled study of safety and efficacy in patients 4 to 12 years of age with ADHD, that includes sparse PK sampling in children 4 to less than 6 years of age; and 2) a 12 month, open-label safety study in children 4 to less than 6 years of age to assess effects on growth.
L185: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Attention Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) is the most common neurobehavioral disorder of childhood, with a lifetime prevalence in the pediatric population of about 11%. It typically presents in early school years and is characterized by difficulty paying attention, hyperactivity, and impulsive behavior. These symptoms cause significant impairment in academic and social functioning during critical years of development, unless treated.	ADHD is a very prevalent condition in children and adolescents. ADHD symptoms can substantially compromise childhood academic and social development without treatment.
L186: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation: NDA 212994
## Azstarys (serdexmethylphenidate and dexmethylphenidate) capsules, for oral use
L187: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	
There are several products that have demonstrated safety and effectiveness in the treatment of ADHD over the past 65 years. Most of these products contain amphetamine salts or methylphenidate. More recently approved products contain atomoxetine or guanfacine. These products display some differences in time to therapeutic onset and/or duration of action because of different pharmacokinetic profiles. Thus, some require more than one dose per day because of a short duration of action. Also, products have been developed as different formulations (tablets, capsules, or oral suspensions) which allow for different modes of oral administration (sprinkling on food, chewing, swallowing whole pills).
KP415 offers no distinct advantage over currently approved methylphenidate products.

Benefit	
Study KP415.E01 and data from the approval of Focalin XR together provide substantial evidence of the efficacy of KP415 in the treatment of children (ages 6 to 12 years) with ADHD. In Study KP415.E01, the average change from baseline in the SKAMP-C score on the laboratory classroom day in the KP415-treated patients was statistically significantly greater than that in the placebo-treated patients. The Agency required only one efficacy trial because the effectiveness of d-MPH has already been established for the LD, Focalin XR. Efficacy in adolescent and adult patients with ADHD is inferred via a PK bridge to the LD, Focalin XR.
The Applicant has provided substantial evidence of the efficacy of KP415 in the treatment of patients, age 6 years and older, with ADHD.

Risk and Risk Management	
Adverse events (AEs) after KP415 administration in phase 3 trials were generally the same as those observed with other MPH products, e.g., decreased appetite, mood changes, insomnia, abdominal pain, headache, and behavioral changes. Possible hypersensitivity reactions to KP415 were bronchospasm, rash, and pruritus. There was a modest increase from baseline in blood pressure and pulse rate during KP415 treatment. During the 12-month safety trial, there was an appreciable
The clinical review identified no findings that suggest a new risk that is reasonably attributable to KP415.
Possible hypersensitivity reactions will be described in Section 4 (Contraindications) of labeling.

There was also a lower than expected increase in height, after adjustment for sex and age, with a mean z-score change from baseline to month 12 of -0.21 SDs among study completers. L193: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L194: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L195: Attention Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) is the most common neurobehavioral disorder of childhood, with a lifetime prevalence in the pediatric population of about 11%. ADHD typically presents in early school years and is characterized by difficulty paying attention, hyperactivity, and impulsive behavior. The clinical presentation may be primarily inattentive, hyperactive/impulsive, or combined. These symptoms cause significant impairment in academic and social functioning during critical years of development, unless treated. Although symptoms often attenuate during adolescence and early adulthood, some patients continue to experience the full disorder or some symptoms of the disorder into mid-adulthood, when symptoms can cause substantial impairment in occupational functioning. Pharmacologic treatments for ADHD have had a significant impact on the well-being and functioning of patients with this disorder.
L196: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L197: Several products have demonstrated safety and efficacy in the treatment of patients with ADHD over the last 65 years. Most of these products contain amphetamine salts or methylphenidate, and are associated with abuse potential, decreased appetite, and insomnia. These products display some differences in time to therapeutic onset and/or duration of action because of different PK profiles. Thus, some require more than one dose per day because of a short duration of action. Also, products have been developed as different formulations (tablets, capsules, or oral suspensions) which allow for different modes of oral administration (sprinkling on food, chewing, swallowing whole pills) to meet the needs of individual patients.
L198: More recently approved products contain atomoxetine or guanfacine. Atomoxetine is a selective presynaptic norepinephrine reuptake inhibitor that has no known abuse potential, but is associated with nausea and decreased appetite. Guanfacine is a central \alpha_{2A} adrenergic receptor agonist that is approved for monotherapy or as adjunctive therapy to stimulants in the treatment of patients with ADHD. Guanfacine has no known abuse potential, but is associated with hypotension, bradycardia, syncope, and somnolence.
L199: KP415 capsules can be administered once daily, based on a duration of action up to 10 hours after dosing. Also, KP415 can be either swallowed whole or opened, with the entire contents sprinkled onto applesauce or added to water. Because other marketed d-MPH products have these features, KP415 offers no distinct advantage over the existing treatment armamentarium.
L200: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L201: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L202: MPH products have been approved and used in the United States to treat ADHD for several decades and have a well-established safety and efficacy profile.
L203: KP415 contains a new molecular entity (NME), SDX, which is a prodrug of d-MPH. Originally, the Applicant intended to develop a product [REDACTED] (b) (4) with the expectation of marketing a treatment for ADHD that had reduced abuse liability via non-oral routes, such as nasal insufflation, based on the fact that SDX requires conversion to d-MPH in the gastrointestinal tract. However, after PK data with SDX showed that peak blood levels of d-MPH are not achieved until about 8 hours after dosing, the Applicant decided [REDACTED] (b) (4) that KP415 would provide a therapeutic effect from the first hour post-dose throughout the day. Thus, as for other MPH products, the Drug Enforcement Administration will likely designate KP415 as a Schedule II product under the Controlled Substances Act.
L204: KP415 has not been marketed in any country.
L205: ## 3.2. Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
L206: KemPharm, Inc. has developed KP415 for the treatment of ADHD. This 505(b)(2) application for KP415 is relying on Focalin XR (NDA 21802) as the LD to bridge to prior findings of safety and efficacy for a once daily d-MPH drug product.
L207: The Agency provided advice to the Applicant under IND 130463 on several occasions during the development of KP415:
L208: - Pre-IND Written Response Only (WRO) comments, dated July 1, 2016, provided advice on nonclinical studies, the design of the initial phase 1 oral bioavailability study, and the 505(b)(2) pathway to approval. Also, the Controlled Substances Staff (CSS) advised the Applicant that the abuse potential of SDX alone must be evaluated, because it is not currently scheduled as a controlled substance.
- The Applicant submitted an IND application on September 6, 2016 and the Agency issued a "May Proceed" letter on November 2, 2016.
- The Agency denied a September 6, 2016, request for Fast Track designation because the Applicant did not demonstrate that an unmet medical need existed. The Agency denied a second request for Fast Track designation on May 15, 2018 for the same reason.
L209: - The Agency held an End-of-Phase 1 meeting on June 14, 2017. The Agency advised the Applicant that additional animal studies may be needed to qualify SDX and any other major human metabolites. Other discussion topics included 1) the potential need to conduct additional PK studies, 2) the design of a PK bridging study, 3) extrapolation of efficacy from children to adolescents, 4) the design of a pivotal efficacy trial in children, and 5) studies to evaluate human abuse potential (HAP).
- The Agency held an End of Phase 2 (EOP2) meeting on November 14, 2017. The Agency indicated that SDX alone is a new molecular entity (NME) that is not rapidly metabolized to d-MPH and, therefore, SDX itself required adequate assessment for toxicity in animals. Other issues that were discussed included 1) the need to compare PK data across child, adolescent, and adult age ranges to support safety and efficacy in patients age 6 years and older, 2) feedback from CSS on proposed HAP studies, and 3) the need to evaluate the potential for alcohol-induced dose dumping. It should be noted that the Applicant asked if their efficacy and safety findings in children (6 to 12 years) from Study KP415.E01 could support safety and efficacy in all patients 6 years of age and older. We informed them that this will be a matter of review after they submit the results of these studies and provide justification why safety and efficacy can be inferred for adolescents and adults based on the available data in children ages 6 to 12 years.
- The Agency issued an Agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) on August 2, 2018.
- Written Response Only (WRO) comments, dated October 10, 2018, addressed concerns regarding the protocol-specified analysis of data supporting therapeutic onset and duration of effect claims from the pivotal efficacy study (Study KP415.E01). The baseline time point specified in the protocol (first day of randomized treatment) differed from that usually implemented in similar laboratory classroom studies (predose assessment on the day of the laboratory classroom study) and yielded results which the Applicant felt did not reflect the true magnitude of onset and duration of therapeutic effect. A post-hoc analysis was claimed to be more accurate. The Division advised the Applicant to discuss this issue within the NDA. The Division's decision on the acceptability of the post-hoc analysis will be a matter for review.
- The Agency held a pre-NDA meeting on April 10, 2019. The Agency informed the Applicant that Study KP415.E01 appears to be adequate to assess the efficacy of SDX/d-MPH in the treatment of ADHD in children 6 to 12 years of age. The Agency also informed the Applicant that the October 2018 justification for the post hoc analysis appeared to be reasonable but the acceptability of this analysis (b) (4) will be a matter for review. The Division noted that, in the post hoc analysis, the mean changes from baseline in the SKAMP-C at 12 and 13 hours post-dose indicated worsening ADHD symptoms in the SDX/d-MPH-treated patients compared to baseline, although the changes were statistically superior to
L210: those in placebo-treated patients. Thus, [REDACTED] (b) (4) these findings should be addressed in the NDA application. The Applicant stated that one possible reason for deterioration of efficacy at the end of the day is fatigue at the end of the laboratory classroom day. Additionally, we informed the Applicant that the extrapolation of efficacy from children (ages 6 to 12 years) to adolescents (ages 13 to 17 years) and to adults (18 years and older) will be a matter for review.
L211: The Applicant submitted NDA 212994 on March 2, 2020. At that time, the study report for Study KP415.S01, a 12-month, open-label safety study in children 6 to 12 years of age, was not complete; only an interim clinical study report (CSR) was submitted, with the full report to be submitted as part of the 120-Day Safety Update Report (SUR) for this application. The Agency agreed with this plan at the pre-NDA meeting. The application was filed on May 11, 2020.
L212: The Applicant submitted the 120-Day SUR for this application on June 18, 2020. This submission included the final study report for Study KP415.S01.
L213: The Agency concluded that the first proposed proprietary name, [REDACTED] (b) (4) was conditionally acceptable on May 29, 2020. The Applicant withdrew the name [REDACTED] on July 30, 2020, and requested review of a new proprietary name, Azstarys, on that same date. The Agency found the name Azstarys to be conditionally acceptable on October 28, 2020.
L214: The Agency held a Mid-Cycle teleconference with the Applicant on August 13, 2020. Significant issues for discussion were 1) abnormal laboratory findings in phase 3 trials (proteinuria and increased coagulation parameters), 2) unexpected efficacy findings from Study KP415.E01, 3) the choice of the baseline time point for the primary efficacy analysis, and 4) the proposed dosing recommendations for labeling.
L215: The Agency held a Late-Cycle teleconference with the Applicant on December 1, 2020. Minor issues for discussion were 1) the PK bridging strategy to support extrapolation of efficacy from children (6 to 12 years of age) to adolescents (13 to 17 years of age) and adults (18 years and older) and 2) the choice of the baseline time point for the primary efficacy analysis. The Agency agreed to advise the Applicant on a Postmarketing Requirement (PMR) to assess PK, safety, and efficacy in 4- and 5-year olds with ADHD after internal Agency discussion, to be conducted in January 2021.
L216: The Division of Psychiatry (DP) conferred with team staff at the Office of Neuroscience (ON) level on February 18, 2021, regarding the limitation of the approved population age range to patients 6 to 12 years of age. After in-depth discussion of this issue and further discussion within DP the next day, DP decided to expand the approved age range to patients age 6 years and older. The rationale for this decision is provided in Section 8.1.2 of this review.

L228: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L229: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L230: This application submitted by Commave Therapeutics SA is a 505(b)(2) NDA for KP415 (serdexamethylphenidate and dexmethylphenidate) for the treatment of Attention Deficit Hyper Activity Disorder (ADHD) in patients ages 6 years and older. KP415 is a combination of a new molecular entity (NME), serdexamethylphenidate (SDX), and a previously approved product, dexmethylphenidate (d-MPH). SDX is a prodrug of d-MPH. The proposed maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) is 52.3/10.4 mg SDX/d-MPH (56/12 mg of SDX Cl/d-MPH Cl). The Applicant is partially relying on the Agency's previous findings of safety for Focalin XR (d-MPH; NDA 021802), a central nervous system (CNS) stimulant, approved in 2005 for the same indication proposed for KP415. The submitted nonclinical package contains studies to support approval of SDX, which is also referred to in these studies as KP415. It should be noted that KP415 in the non-clinical section refers only to the prodrug SDX and not to the combination of SDX with d-MPH.
L231: SDX did not show significant binding to the human transporters for dopamine, norepinephrine, and serotonin, or to 68 other molecular targets examined.
L232: There are substantial species differences in pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicokinetics (TK) for SDX and the active metabolite, d-MPH. After single and repeat dosing with SDX, rodents (rats and mice) have greater plasma exposures (AUC and C_{max}) to d-MPH compared to SDX, while dogs and rabbits have lower plasma exposures to d-MPH compared to SDX. In addition, rabbits reach maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) for d-MPH the slowest after single and repeat doses of SDX (approximately 2 to 4 hours in rabbits compared to 0.5 to 1.2 hours in rats and 0.5 to 2 hours in dogs).
L233: SDX has minimal distribution or binding to red blood cells. [^{14}C]SDX-related radioactivity widely distributed in rat tissues with the highest radioactivity concentrations observed in eye uvea, kidney cortex and medulla, liver, gastric mucosa of stomach, and small intestine. SDX was extensively metabolized in a rodent mass balance study where only trace amounts of SDX were detected in plasma and feces. The active metabolite d-MPH was only detected in plasma and the inactive metabolite ritalinic acid was detected in plasma, urine, and feces. SDX is thought to release d-MPH by hydrolysis at the serine moiety (producing the intermediate SDX-des-Ser) or at the carbamate group (producing the intermediate nicotinoyl-L-Ser). SDX-des-Ser was identified at low levels (1.33% of total dose) in feces of humans in a mass balance study but was not observed in rats or dogs. The low level of this metabolite in human feces is not of a safety concern. Nicotinoyl-L-Ser was present in pooled plasma samples from rats and dogs but not in any human matrix. Release of d-MPH from SDX is predicted to result in the release of formaldehyde and the final products of SDX metabolism are L-Ser and niacin. These amounts are either found endogenously and/or obtained in the diet at levels greater than what will be
L234: released at the MRHD and are not of a safety concern. No unique metabolites of d-MPH were observed.
L235: All findings from safety pharmacology studies of SDX were consistent with known effects for d-MPH. In rat and dog single- and repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to 28-day duration of oral SDX with d-MPH as a comparator, the dose-limiting toxicities were clinical signs consistent with a CNS stimulant [including increased activity, excessive licking or grooming (which could lead to self-mutilation), and/or increased respiration] and decreases in body weight gain or body weight loss. All findings in SDX dose groups were similar to findings in the d-MPH comparator group for both 28-day studies; therefore, no unique SDX toxicity was observed that could be attributed to either the parent or the unique metabolites of SDX. For rats, the exposures at the no adverse effect level (NOAEL) for stimulant-related findings (25 mg/kg) was less than the human exposure at the MRHD. For dogs, the NOAEL for stimulant-related findings was not established because the NOAEL is less than the lowest dose tested.
L236: Because SDX is an NME that is not rapidly metabolized to d-MPH in humans (half-life of SDX is ~5.7 hours) and will be used chronically, SDX needed to be adequately assessed for toxicity. In order to use the species with the highest SDX exposure compared to humans in the pivotal embryo-fetal development study and the 6-month juvenile animal study, a cross-species analysis was conducted, and the rabbit was determined to be the most appropriate species.
L237: In the embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, there were no findings of maternal or fetal development toxicity at exposures up to 49 times (400 mg/kg) the MRHD. Because of the large exposure multiple, the high dose was considered adequate for the embryo-fetal development study even though it was not the maximum tolerated dose (MTD). In a dose range-finding study in juvenile rabbits, adverse clinical signs (including excessive licking, grooming, and scratching that resulted in self-mutilation) were observed at doses \ge 400 mg/kg and the MTD was considered 300 mg/kg. In the pivotal 6-month juvenile animal study in rabbits, clinical signs consistent with a stimulant were mild and not considered adverse at all doses tested. The NOAEL (300 mg/kg) in juvenile rabbits is 50-times the MRHD for juveniles based on body surface area (no human plasma levels at MRHD in juveniles were available). Of note, d-MPH exposures after a single-dose of 400 mg/kg SDX CI in juvenile rabbits was 3-times the d-MPH exposures after a single-dose of 400 mg/kg SDX CI in pregnant rabbits which might account for the observance of adverse clinical signs in juvenile rabbits but no clinical signs in pregnant rabbits.
L238: SDX was not genotoxic in an appropriate valid battery of assays. After a positive prediction of mutagenicity by QSAR for an impurity (chloro-substituted SDX, CI-SDX or CI-KP415), a valid Ames assay determined that CI-SDX was negative for mutagenicity.
L239: There are no novel excipients in the drug product. Drug substance impurity and drug product degradant specifications and elemental impurities risk assessment are consistent with ICH guidelines.
L240: Although SDX is technically a new molecular entity and circulating levels of SDX can be detected in humans at levels comparable to the active d-MPH, during development, the Division agreed to consider an abbreviated nonclinical drug development program if adequate data were provided to support the conclusion that the toxicological profile of SDX is comparable to d-MPH and there are no unique toxicities derived from the parent or the unique metabolic byproducts released during prodrug metabolism to d-MPH. The Division agreed to review 28-day repeat-dose toxicology data in two species, an embryo-fetal development study in the rabbit, and 6-month juvenile rabbit study to support this position. Upon review, it was concluded that additional nonclinical studies are not needed for SDX because there were no unique, adverse findings for SDX compared to d-MPH in 28-day repeat-dose toxicology studies in rat and dog, embryo-fetal development study in rabbits, and 6-month juvenile animal study in rabbits, the unique metabolic by products do not raise any safety concerns, and further studies would not provide any additional data not already characterized via referenced data for d-MPH. In addition, carcinogenicity studies in mice or rats, fertility and early embryonic development in rats, embryofetal development in rats, and prenatal and postnatal development study in rats would not be informative because of species differences in pharmacokinetics for SDX (see review for more details).
L241: **Recommendation:** The Applicant has provided sufficient nonclinical safety information on serdexamethylphenidate to support approval for the treatment of ADHD in patients ages 6 years and older from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective.
L242: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L243: NDA 021802 for Focalin XR (d-MPH)
L244: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L245: Serdexamethylphenidate (SDX; KP415) is a prodrug of d-methylphenidate hydrochloride (d-MPH). SDX contains a molecule of d-MPH connected to a nicotinoyl-L-serine (nicotinoyl-L-Ser) by a carboxymethylene linker (Figure 1).
L246: See Figure 22, page 128 in Appendix for the proposed metabolic pathway. Although the Applicant did not measure plasma exposures for formaldehyde, L-Ser, and niacin in humans and animals after SDX administration; this is not of a safety concern because they are either found endogenously in humans (formaldehyde and L-Ser), are found in foods (formaldehyde, L-Ser, and niacin), in dietary supplements (L-Ser and niacin), or approved drug products (niacin) at levels above those found in SDX (see Table 43, page 123 in Appendix). Therefore, this review of SDX safety will not further discuss formaldehyde, L-Ser, and niacin.

L255: ## *Primary and Secondary Pharmacology*
L256: In a primary pharmacology in vitro radioligand receptor binding assay (Study No. AB54825); SDX at concentrations from 0.03 to 10 \muM did not show significant binding to the human dopamine transporter (DAT), norepinephrine transporter (NET), or serotonin transporter (SERT; IC_{50} &gt; 10 \muM for all three transporters). Of the three human transporters, SDX appeared to have higher affinity for DAT and inhibited 33% and 44% of ligand binding at 3 and 10 \muM, respectively (Table 1). The validity of this assay was confirmed with reference compounds which generated IC_{50} values comparable to historical control values. In a secondary pharmacology radioligand binding screening assay with 68 primary molecular targets including CNS targets (Study No. AB54826), SDX at 10 \muM showed limited affinity for the targets investigated (&lt;25% inhibition). Notably, at 10 \muM, SDX had 41% inhibition of radioligand binding to DAT, consistent with the value from primary pharmacology study confirming that there is minimal activity at the DAT. In addition, no significant binding was observed with the nicotinoyl-L-Ser (10 \muM) moiety in a radioligand binding screening assay with 68 molecular targets (Study No. AB54826).

L260: ## *Safety Pharmacology*
L261: SDX was evaluated in a standard battery of safety pharmacology studies which included the comparator d-MPH (CNS study No. 0200RK19.001, hERG assay study No. CYP1555-R3, cardiovascular study No., and respiratory study No. 1275RK19.001). All findings for SDX were typical of a CNS stimulant (including dilated pupil, increased activity, increased heart rate and blood pressure, and increased respiratory rate) and all findings were generally comparable for SDX and a comparative dose of d-MPH. In the hERG assay, the IC50 for SDX was considered &gt;25 µM.

Distribution
(Study Nos. CY1555-R2, CYP1555-R5, and 437N-1701)
SDX and d-MPH moderately bound to human plasma protein (56% and 47.3%, respectively). SDX has minimal distribution or binding to red blood cells [mean blood-to-plasma concentration ratio (C_b/C_{pl}) = 0.59; mean red blood cell partition coefficient (K_{RBC/PL}) = 0.01].
[^{14}C]KP415-related radioactivity was widely distributed in rat tissues with peak radioactive concentration in blood and most tissues 1-hour postdose. Quantifiable concentrations of radioactivity were present in most tissues through 48 hours post dose with half-life ranging from <10 hours (22 of 42 tissues sampled) to >50 hours (eye uvea, thyroid, kidney cortex, and liver). The half-life in blood was 4.7 hours. The highest radioactivity concentrations were found in eye uvea, kidney cortex, liver, kidney medulla, stomach (gastric mucosa), and small intestine.

Metabolism
In Vitro (Study Nos. 437D-1601, CYP1555-R1, CYP1555-R4, CYP1555-R6, CYP1555-R7A, CYP1555-R7D, CYP1555-R7E, CYP1555-R7F, and CYP1555-R7G)
SDX was slowly metabolized in human whole blood, plasma, simulated intestinal fluid (SIF), simulated gastric fluid (SGF), human kidney S9 fractions, human liver S9 fractions, human intestinal S9 fractions, and human lung S9 fractions. In blood, the amount of SDX after 90 minutes of incubation was 73.8%. d-MPH was first observed at 45 mins after incubation with whole blood and plasma, at 90 mins with SIF, and was not detected in SGF.
SDX did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4 at concentrations up to 30 \muM and did not inhibit UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9 and UGT2B7 at concentrations up to 100 \muM. SDX did not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 at concentrations up to 100 \muM. SDX was not found to be metabolized by CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A4/5, CYP2A6, CYP2 E1, UGT1A1, UGT1A3,
L266: Type of Study	Major Findings
UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT2B7 and UGT2B15, aldehyde oxidase, xanthine oxidase, and monoamine oxidase.
In Vivo (Study No. 4001435, KP415-MP-001, and 4006354)	
Thirty-four putative metabolites of SDX were identified from plasma collected from rats dosed with 114.87 mg/kg SDX. Four metabolites resulted from hydrolysis at the serine moiety [M25 (SDX-des-Ser)], the carbamate moiety [M1 (nicotinoyl-L-Ser) and M2a (d-MPH)], and at the methyl ester [M33 (SDX acid)]. Twenty-six reflect metabolism of d-MPH via oxidation, reductive elimination, or hydrolysis, and four appear to be glucuronide conjugates of oxidized d-MPH.
Results from a rodent mass balance study, rats orally dosed with 75 mg/kg (184.5 µCi/kg) [14C]-SDX Cl, confirmed that SDX is extensively metabolized in rats (Table 44, page 125 in Appendix). Trace amounts of SDX were detected in plasma and feces. The active metabolite, d-MPH was only detected in plasma. The inactive metabolite RA was detected in plasma, urine, and feces.
In rats and dogs; nicotinoyl-L-Ser, SDX acid, and ritalinic acid (RA; M3a, a known metabolite of MPH) were present in pooled plasma after a single oral and 28-day oral repeat dose of SDX, while SDX-des-Ser (M25) was below the level of quantification at both time points. In humans who received 32 mg oral SDX, only ritalinic acid was present in pooled plasma samples. See Table 45, page 127 in Appendix for PK data.
The proposed metabolic pathway of SDX is shown in Figure 22, page 128 in Appendix.

Excretion
(Study No. 437N-1701)	
The primary route of excretion in rat was urine with some elimination in feces. After administration of a single oral dose of 75 mg/kg [14C]KP415 in male rats, 64.9% of radioactivity was measured in urine and 30.3% of radioactivity was measured in feces. Approximately 94% of the radioactivity was recovered within 24 h post dose.

L271: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L272: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L273: ## A 28-Day Oral Toxicity Study with KP415 in Rats Followed by a 14-Day Recovery Period/Study No. 0436RK19.003:
L274: - A dose-dependent increase in severity and/or incidence of clinical signs consistent with a stimulant, including increased activity, biting and licking of the home cage, excessive grooming, and head twitching.
- Decreased body weight gain (up to 20% for males and 33% for females) at the end of dosing consistent with a stimulant.
- Findings in SDX dose groups were similar to findings in the d-MPH comparator group.
- The NOAEL is 25 mg/kg based on the moderate to severe clinical signs at 100/75 mg/kg and decreased body weight gain at doses \ge50 mg/kg, which is less than the human exposure at the MRHD (AUC_{24hr,ss} = 241 ng.h/mL).
L275: (b) (4)
L276: Conducting laboratory and location:
L277: GLP compliance: Yes
L278: ## Methods

Once daily for 28 days
Route of administration:	Oral gavage
Formulation/Vehicle:	Solution/deionized water
Species/Strain:	Rat/Sprague-Dawley
Number/Sex/Group:	15/sex/group for control, HD, and d-MPH (5/sex/group recovery group); 10/sex/group for LD and MD
Age:	7 - 8 weeks
Satellite groups/ unique design:	9/sex/dose group and d-MPH (TK)
No unique study design
Deviation from study protocol
affecting interpretation of results:	Yes
L280: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose
L281: ^Because of adverse clinical signs, the doses were lowered for the SDX high-dose group from 100 to 75 mg/kg and d-MPH comparator group from 50 to 37.5 mg/kg on Day 3 for males and Day 2 for females.
L282: ## Observations and Results: changes from control
L283: ## NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation: NDA 212994
## Azstarys (serdexamethylphenidate and dexmethylphenidate) capsules, for oral use
L284: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	There were no test article-related deaths. There were 2 d-MPH related deaths (1 male from the d-MPH TK group was found dead on Day 5 and one female from the d-MPH TK group was euthanized because of clinical signs on Day 1).
Clinical Signs	Test article- and d-MPH-related clinical signs including increased activity, biting and licking of the home cage, excessive grooming, swollen or missing digits (probably from self-mutilation), scabs on various locations on the body (which were often accompanied by red substance), and head twitching were observed. The severity and/or incidence generally occurred in a dose-dependent manner with moderate to severe findings at the HD. No clinical signs were noted during the recovery period.
Body Weights	There were test article- and d-MPH related decreases in body weight gain over the dosing period consistent with a stimulant (% of control gain: 99.6 LDM, 80 MDM, 82 HDM, 75 d-MPHM, 91 LDF, 78 MDF, 67 HDF, 79 d-MPHF). During the recovery period, HD and d-MPH males and females had the same amount of body weight gain or increased body weight gain compared to controls, but HD and d-MPH males still weighed less than the control group at the end of the recovery period.
Ophthalmoscopy	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Hematology	A small, but significant decrease in mean white blood cell (\downarrow20%), absolute neutrophil (\downarrow30%), and absolute monocyte (\downarrow37%) counts were seen on Day 29 for HDM and d-MPH comparators compared to controls. Neutrophil and monocyte counts were also decreased for MDM compared to controls (\downarrow28% and 35%, respectively). There was no difference after the recovery period.
Clinical Chemistry	A dose-related increase in mean glucose (up to \uparrow29%) and sodium (up to \uparrow2%) was observed on Day 29 for males and females and for d-MPH comparator. Increase in mean total bilirubin (up to \uparrow 18%) for MDM, HDM (not statistically significant) and d-MPHM observed on Day 29. Increase in mean alkaline phosphatase for d-MPHM (\uparrow32%) on Day 29. Decrease in mean creatinine levels (up to \downarrow12%) for MDF, HDF, and d-MPHF on Day 29. No differences were seen after the recovery period.
Urinalysis	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Gross Pathology	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
L285: Organ Weights	Increases in absolute and relative liver (up to ↑23%) were observed for all female dose groups and d-MPH comparator on Day 29. Increases in absolute and relative kidney weights (up to ↑9%) were observed for HD and d-MPH females at Day 29. No differences were observed after the recovery period.
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
L286: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose.

L296: ## **Observations and Results: changes from control**
L297: Parameters	Major findings
Mortality	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related deaths.
Clinical Signs	Test article- and d-MPH-related clinical signs included increased activity, repetitive rapid movements from side to side, sudden intermittent movement, increased respiration, excessive licking, vocalization and small to medium amount of salivation. Frequency and severity of clinical signs increased with increasing dose.
Body Weights	Test article- and d-MPH-dosed dogs gained less weight compared to controls or lost weight over the dosing period [mean gain (kg) from Day 1 to Day 28: 0.29 control M, 0 LDM, 0.1 MDM, 0 HDM, -0.4 d-MPHM, 0.32 control F, -0.04 LDF, 0.06 MDF, -0.26 HDF, -0.58 d-MPHF]. HD and d-MPH males and females gained more weight compared to controls during the recovery period [mean gain (kg) 0.2 control M, 0.35 HDM, 0.3 d-MPHM, -0.1 control F, 0.55 HDF, 0.5 d-MPHF]. In general, decreased body weight gain correlated with decreased food consumption at timepoints throughout the dosing period and increased body weight gain correlated with increased food consumption at timepoints throughout the recovery period.
Ophthalmoscopy	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
ECG	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Hematology	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Clinical Chemistry	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Urinalysis	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Gross Pathology	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
Organ Weights	Increase in relative liver weight (\uparrow24%) was observed for HD and d-MPH males on Day 29, but not after the recovery period.
Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes	There was no test article- or d-MPH-related findings.
L298: LD: low dose; MD: mid dose; HD: high dose.
L299: ## **General toxicology; additional studies**
L300: Single-dose and 14-day oral gavage studies were conducted in rats [single doses \le800 mg/kg (Study No. 0406RK19.004) and 14-day doses \le 48.8 mg/kg (Study No. 0437RK19.007)] and dogs [single doses \le120 mg/kg (Study No. 0433DK19.004) and 14-day doses \le9.8 mg/kg (Study No. 0437DK19.006)]. Findings were consistent with those observed with d-MPH (including hyperactivity, cage licking, excessive biting and self-mutilation).
L301: **Reviewer comment:** Findings from general toxicology studies up to 28-days in duration in 2 species confirm that in rat and dog the dose-limiting toxicities for SDX administration are known stimulant effects attributed to d-MPH. No unique toxicities were observed that could be attributed to either SDX or a unique metabolite of SDX. However, even though SDX is a prodrug of an approved product (d-MPH), we determined that a chronic study adequately assessing SDX toxicity in an appropriate species was needed because SDX is an NME that has a longer half-life in humans (~5.7 hours) compared to other species (~0.5-2 hours). We determined that this study could be conducted in juvenile animals to cover the pediatric indication. Based on the Applicant's cross-species analysis, rabbit was determined to be the most appropriate species to characterize the effects of SDX (see Species Justification for Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology Studies, Section 1.5.4, for more details). Because no unique toxicities were identified in the chronic juvenile toxicology study in rabbits (study reviewed under Reproductive and Development Toxicology Section, Section 1.5.4, for more details), we conclude that a chronic toxicology study in a second species is not needed for SDX.
